Apple’s iPod Turns 25: Here’s the Song Catalog That Rode Wit You

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

On this day in 2001, Apple's iconic MP3 player was released. And now, an Apple Music easter egg lets you experience the nostalgic sound and scroll of the gadget's click wheel.

Back in 2001, some of the top songs included Nelly’s Ride Wit Me, Fallin’ by Alicia Keys and Ms. Jackson by Outkast. When people wanted to listen to those songs but weren’t near a car stereo or their home audio equipment, most carried music with them on skip-prone portable CD players, boomboxes or maybe even a homemade mix cassette.

Sure, some early adopters were already carrying a small collection of songs on a portable MP3 player (MP3s: the new-new thing!) like the Diamond Rio or Creative’s Nomad Jukebox. But these weren’t common, and you didn’t see them that often. There was no ubiquitous sign yet that the times were changing musically in a digital direction.

Enter Apple in November 2001 with its original iPod, a small hard drive encased in smooth white plastic that could stash between 1,000 and 2,000 songs, a sum that seemed godly at the time.

Who would ever need to listen to 1,000 songs (or have access to that many) at their fingertips? What mortal would dare?

The iPod, which was announced on Oct. 23, 2001, and released on Nov. 10, 2001, had a starting price of $400. It heralded the smartphone era before smartphones were a thing, making portable electronics more tactile, user-friendly and information-forward. The white earbuds that were bundled with the device were the beacon of that era. Before long, it seemed everyone had white cables coming out of their ears.

For the 25th anniversary of Apple’s game-changing music player, the device’s creators are getting nostalgic about its silver anniversary. On Apple Music’s website, you can find at its 404 Not Found page a playful mockup of the OG iPod with its pleasing-to-use scroll wheel and a selection of songs you might have had in your iPod at some time or another.

CNET writers and editors had a good time playing around with the fully functional iPod online, sharing favorite hits from its music library and enjoying some nostalgia ASMR.

We also looked back at CNET’s earlier write-ups, including one from CNET’s former Editor-in-Chief Connie Guglielmo, explaining what it was like to watch Steve Jobs introduce the iPod. There’s also a great 2021 piece by CNET’s Scott Stein on the iPod’s evolution, and a commentary by CNET’s Ty Pendlebury on how the iPod captured and held the throne for so long, surviving music streaming and rival players.

Read more: iPhone Duo Review: Late to the Foldable Party, but It Steals the Show

If the iPod can be primarily credited to one person — leading a team of many — it’s widely acknowledged to be Tony Fadell, who came to Apple as a contractor and went on to head one of its most successful product divisions. These days, Fadell is thinking a lot about the future of AI assistant devices, but he took time on his LinkedIn to acknowledge the iPod anniversary this week.

“We took it from approval to shipping in less than eight months,” he wrote. “There wasn’t time for endless refinements. We had to make decisions, trust our experience and stay focused on one clear promise: 1,000 songs in your pocket.”

While Fadell says that the iPod took a few generations to really take off and eventually come in iterations like the Nano and the Touch, some music fans who still love the original iPod design have been using the device for decades. If you have any hard drive-based gadget that lasts that long, count yourself lucky.

In addition to those old enough to remember what a Hoobastank is, iPods are also getting popular again with younger generations on a nostalgia kick, many of whom would rather carry around a separate physical device for their music than stream their Spotify accounts on their phone for the millionth time. Axios reports that iPods and MP3s are back in a big way in a trend called “friction-maxxing.” You don’t have to use that term to indulge, we promise.

Getting into the hobby isn’t cheap: Working iPod models can cost several hundred dollars on eBay. And one that’s never been opened? Try $25,000 to $60,000.

A cheaper route may be to keep the familiar outside trappings but replace the guts with a new motherboard. A group called Crowd Supply promises in a YouTube video a new way to bring nonworking iPods back to life. It hasn’t launched yet, but you can keep an eye on the project at Crowd Supply’s website. Other tinkerers have had years and years to work up hundreds of iPod hacks; you can find many of them on Hackaday.

Alternatively, it may be the function that a modern MP3 enthusiast seeks, not necessarily the Apple branding or hardware. Other companies, such as Fosi Audio, have their own takes on the modern music player, like the $80 DAP Neo. CNET has a list of the best MP3 players of 2026, with devices like the Shanling M0s and the Android-based HiBy R4 coming out on top.

Lastly, if you want to relive the iPod glory days, you don’t necessarily need new (or old) hardware at all; your phone can do it with iOS apps such as RetroPod, My Classic – Retro Console or, if you’re feeling saucy on Android, ClassiPod.

→ Baca artikel asli

Tesla 'Full Self-Driving' One Step Closer To Recreating Human Driving By Overtaking Bicycles, Exceeding Speed Limits And Misidentifying Road Signs

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Humans are imperfect, so at least in theory, robotaxis are supposed to save us by doing all the perfect driving we aren't able to do. Of course, had we poured every dollar that's been spent on robotaxi development into improving public transportation, self-driving cars would feel unnecessary. But the bigger problem is, after all those billions of dollars and years of development, Momentum Mag reports that a recent test of Tesla's so-called "Full Self-Driving" software found that it loved to speed through neighborhoods, illegally pass cyclists, and break other traffic laws. So basically, it drives like your least-favorite neighbor.

Woke 3 already killed the Cybertruck, so Tesla's school bus-passing software could be next on the chopping block after liberals figure out how to make every man a little shorter every time he disappoints a woman. Until then, it's important to know how good or bad the software controlling some of the most popular new cars actually is, which is why Belgian mobility organization johanna.be put the software to the test and found it frequently breaks speed limit laws in areas where drivers are most likely to hit pedestrians or cyclists. So that's not great.

Additionally, while there aren't many places in the U.S. where drivers can't legally pass a cyclist, that isn't the case in Belgium. You'd think fine-tuning the software to account for changes in laws between countries would have been a pretty basic part of Tesla's development, but nope. In johanna.be's testing, it found that Tesla's FSD system "repeatedly tries to overtake cyclists in cycle streets, something that is explicitly illegal in Belgium."

Coincidentally, right around the time johanna.be published the results of its Tesla safety test, the European Transport Safety Council shared the results of a separate safety test. The ETSC found that Tesla's "speed offset" and "contextual max speed" features don't follow United Nations or European Union rules regarding safety, and it's called on member states to reject both features later this year when the EU plans to hold an important vote on advanced driver assistance system regulation.

The ECF is also asking the EU to create a European Road Safety Agency that would act as "an independent EU body able to investigate issues involving automated systems and share the subsequent findings with other Member States." Even before the ETSC got involved, johanna.be's findings were concerning enough to warrant some serious attention from international regulators that Elon Musk wasn't able to gut. As the ECF put it, Tesla's so-called "Full Self-Driving" software (emphasis is the ECF's):

If a human loved to drive at 150% of the posted speed limit, regularly told people the speed limit was higher than it was, and kept trying to make illegal passes, we'd take their license and tell them to take the bus. With robotaxis and cars with driver-assist systems, it should be the same. Until they can prove they actually follow the rules of the road, there's no reason to let them on our roads. We also shouldn't allow automakers to sell driver-assistance systems that haven't been proven safe, either. Even if you disagree, I don't think you need a PhD in traffic safety to understand the argument there.

That said, it's entirely possible that safety researchers are criticizing Tesla for mistakes that every automaker's software makes. The problem with that argument is that johanna.be ran the same test with a Citroën C3 that managed to correctly identify all seven speed limit signs. For comparison, the Tesla got six of the seven speed limits wrong. It only got one correct. Imagine not only being wrong, but being more wrong than the French. Self-immolation is probably the only option.

While we wait to see how the EU's member states vote, the ECF also noted that otherwise docile behavior is no excuse for Tesla's software behaving badly in other ways. As it noted:

the study also found that FSD behaved cautiously and courteously around pedestrians and cyclists in general, yielding early, giving space, and even slowing for a sleepy cat in the road! However, this should not compensate for a pattern of speeding and illegal overtaking in streets that are primarily designed for safe walking and cycling

Why Aging Stars Suffer A Sudden Luminosity Dip

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

From a naked eye perspective, stars are stable and predictable. Not much goes on but shining. The Sun itself is fairly plane and nondescript and will shine relatively steadily for billions of years. It's considered a low-mass star compared to the most massive stars, which are rarer and contain many times its mass.

Powerful telescopes bring millions of stars of different types and ages into view. Astrophysicists and astronomers watch the Sun and its low-mass brethren, and note changes in their shining, just as they do all types of stars. Their observations reveal the changes stars experience during their long lifetimes.

As low-mass stars like the Sun exhaust their hydrogen supplies and evolve, they become Red Giant Branch (RGB) stars. This is not only theoretical, it's an observational conclusion. RGB stars are easy to observe and study for two reasons: they're bright, and they're numerous. Arcturus, Aldebaran, and Pollus are all bright, naked-eye stars and are all RGB stars. (Well-known Betelgeuse is a Red Supergiant, not quite the same.)

As RGB stars age and cool, they swell in size and become more luminous. It's a pretty steady change. But for some reason, there's a brief and abrupt dip in their brightness known as the RGB luminosity bump. The bump is called that because of how it appears in the colourâmagnitude diagrams of stellar clusters.

Astrophysicists know why the bump ends. But what initiates the bump is still unexplained. Astrophysicists don't like this gap in their knowledge and they've been trying to fill it in.

New research in The Astrophysical Journal Letters has an explanation for the bump. It's titled "The Role of Specific Entropy in the Onset of the Luminosity Bump," and the sole author is Saskia Hekker. Hekker is a Professor of Theoretical Astrophysics and the Group Leader of the Theory and Observations of Stars Group at the Heidelberg Institute for Theoretical Studies.

"During their evolution, stars follow a distinct path in luminosityâtemperature space. Low-mass stars that have exhausted hydrogen in their core follow the so-called red-giant branch (RGB), which is predominantly in the direction of increasing luminosity and decreasing surface temperature," Hekker explains. "The luminosity bump is a temporary decrease in luminosity against this otherwise increasing trend."

The inset grey box is the Hertzsprung-Russel diagram for a 1 solar mass star progressing from the subgiant branch to the tip of the Red Giant Branch. The tiny grey box inside it is magnified in the larger part of the image, highlighting the RGB luminosity bump. Image Credit: S. Hekker 2026 ApJL 1008 L61

The bump is understood theoretically and also confirmed with observations. But there's one discrepancy between theory and observation. In models, the bump appears at higher luminosities than the observed bump, meaning it appears later. "To understand why the bump appears later in the models than in observations, the physics of the bump needs to be unravelled," Hekker writes.

Stars, including our Sun, are layered, and RGB stars are no exception. But unlike the Sun, RGB stars have exhausted the hydrogen in their cores, and most of what's left is in their envelopes. Since the core has exhausted its hydrogen by fusing it into helium, the core is inert helium. An RGB's fusion occurs in a thin shell. In both these regions, energy is transported via radiation.

Above this radiative region is a convective outer envelope.

As an RGB star ascends the branch, the core contracts and the surface expands. Since the core is inert helium, it exerts no outward pressure and is forced inward. "This causes the deepest layer of the convective envelope to move inwards, reaching layers that have changed their chemical composition due to (burning) processes earlier in the evolution of the star," Hekker explains.

This is when dredge-up occurs, as deeper layers are forced into the convective zone. Since the convective zone is convective, the material from the radiative zone mixes into the convective zone, changing its composition.

Now there's a situation where there's a shifting boundary deep inside the star called the Base of the Convective Zone (BCZ). It's organized around a thermodynamic property called specific entropy. It basically measures how disordered the gas is at the BCZ. As the BCZ moves deeper inside the star, the entropy difference across the boundary shrinks. Past a certain point, entropy across the boundary layer drops past a critical value, and that triggers the luminosity bump.

This diagram shows the interior of an RGB star (not to scale). An inert helium core is in the center, and it's contained in a thin hydrogen-burning shell. Above that is a radiative zone separated from the star's convective envelope by the base of the convective zone (BCZ). Earlier dredge-up and mixing created this boundary between helium-enriched and hydrogen-rich gas. This work ties the RGB luminosity bump to this boundary. The author argues that the onset of the bump occurs when the entropy across this boundary crosses a critical value. (Image Generated with AI)

Previous explanations for the bump rested on mechanics. In those explanations, the thin hydrogen-burning shell reaches the boundary and something inside the star changes, triggering the bump. In this explanation, the bump happens when an entropy threshold is crossed inside the star's thermodynamics. It's not simply that layers meet.

We can't see inside stars. Astrophysicists use physics to try to understand what's going on there, and they test those physics against the only part of the star they can really see, the outside. It's a constant back and forth to refine theories until they match observations.

âThis succinct explanation of the onset of the bump, is the first quantitative description proposed so far,â said Hekker.

This new work will make its way into the larger body of astrophysical theory.

"This viable description of the mechanism behind the onset of the bump provides crucial and new insights for the exploitation of the bump as an anchor point in stellar evolution theory," Hekker writes. "Such added knowledge of the onset of the bump could potentially be used to make more informed choices for changes to the physics in stellar models that go beyond calibration of the overshoot, to place the bump in stellar evolution models at the correct luminosity while at the same time aligning them with measured surface abundances."

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

Think Bigger!! Schwartz Calls for Higher XRP Price as 37 European Banks Embrace Ripple

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

David Schwartz has delivered a fresh bullish message to frustrated XRP holders, saying that a higher price could make the network more efficient. The Ripple CTO believes XRP has more to gain from successful XRP Ledger adoption than its current market valuation.

“The higher the price, the better,” Schwartz wrote in response to a community member questioning whether XRP could support large payment volumes. He explained that higher valuations could make liquidity more cost-effective while reducing slippage during transactions.

The higher the price, the better. A higher price would tend to mean liquidity is more cost-effective and slippage is less.

His comments arrive as XRP trades near $1.10, leaving investors frustrated after another significant market correction. However, developments across Europe’s banking sector are giving the long-term adoption argument fresh momentum.

Schwartz’s latest comments address one of the biggest questions surrounding XRP’s investment thesis: whether the token can capture value as the XRP Ledger expands.

A higher XRP price could make the asset more practical for institutions transferring substantial amounts of money. Each token would represent greater value, potentially reducing the amount of XRP needed for large settlements.

More importantly, deeper liquidity could allow institutions to execute larger transactions without moving the market. That makes XRP’s potential role as a bridge asset particularly interesting for cross-border payments.

Schwartz also offered a strong defense of XRP’s long-term value proposition.

I really do think it's hard to believe we will end up in a world where XRPL is successful but XRP fails to capture a good fraction of that value.

That statement gives XRP holders a bigger reason to watch network adoption beyond daily price movements. Schwartz also acknowledged that XRP’s price currently follows broader cryptocurrency market conditions more closely than developments specific to Ripple and the XRP Ledger.

Nevertheless, his comments reinforce the argument that successful network adoption could eventually benefit the underlying token.

The institutional side of the story is becoming increasingly interesting as European banks prepare to expand blockchain-based financial services.

Qivalis, a consortium of 37 European financial institutions across 15 countries, is preparing to introduce a regulated euro-denominated stablecoin.

The initiative aims to establish a shared digital euro infrastructure for payments and settlements. It could also help European banks compete with dollar-backed stablecoins dominating the global market.

The consortium received Electronic Money Institution authorization from the Dutch central bank in October 2026, bringing its planned stablecoin closer to commercial launch.

Two particularly interesting members are BBVA and DZ BANK, both of which have existing relationships with Ripple’s institutional custody business.

BBVA announced an agreement with Ripple in September 2025 to use Ripple Custody technology for its digital asset services. DZ BANK has also worked with Ripple on institutional digital asset custody infrastructure.

Their participation in Qivalis highlights how established European banks are increasingly exploring blockchain-based financial products.

The immediate market picture remains challenging, but derivatives data offers an interesting contrast. XRP is trading under $1.10, following a sharp correction that pushed the token toward its lowest levels since August.

Binance’s XRP cumulative volume delta recently turned negative, indicating that aggressive market selling exceeded buying activity. This suggests buyers have yet to absorb the latest wave of selling pressure.

Liquidations have also weighed on sentiment. XRP recorded approximately $26 million in liquidations across seven exchanges on November 9, with long positions accounting for 98.4% of the total. Meanwhile, US spot XRP ETFs recorded approximately $10.4 million in net outflows on November 9.

However, these figures do not erase the longer-term institutional developments surrounding Ripple. The growing demand for regulated digital asset infrastructure could create opportunities for XRP as financial institutions explore more efficient ways to move value.

Schwartz’s argument is ultimately about looking beyond the token’s immediate market performance. If the XRP Ledger captures a meaningful share of institutional payments and settlement activity, XRP could become increasingly important as a liquidity asset.

AMD VP and chief Zen architect rebuke claims that Nvidia and Arm made CPUs for agentic AI — ‘We didn’t magically know four years ago that agentic was going to be a thing’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

AMD’s new Venice chips aren’t agentic CPUs, but they’re the best CPUs for agentic AI, says AMD.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Two prominent voices within AMD agree that the company didn’t design its new Zen 6 family, codenamed Venice, explicitly for agentic AI in a clear shot at Nvidia and Arm, who’ve both described their latest data center CPUs as “agentic” in nature. In separate talks, AMD’s chief Zen architect Mike Clark and corporate VP Madhu Rangarajan said that the company didn’t predict the agentic wave when Zen 6 development started.

We just wrapped up testing AMD's Venice CPUs, which you can read more about in our Zen 6 benchmarking article.

CPU development cycles are measured in years, and given that the agentic frenzy around CPUs hit around March of this year, it’s unreasonable to expect that anyone has developed silicon specifically for agentic AI, much less a general-purpose data center CPU. And still, Nvidia claims its Vera CPU was “purpose-built” for agentic AI, while Arm says its AGI CPU was “designed for the demands of agentic AI.”

“We think that the EPYC 9006 CPU is the best CPU for the agentic era, and spoiler alert, we didn’t magically know four years ago that agentic was going to be a thing and purpose-built something for agentic,” said Rangarajan in a session with media. “The reality is an agentic workflow is a diverse set of tasks… and in order to execute those tasks well, you need compute that is able to handle a diverse set of workloads with a full portfolio.”

Introducing the Zen 6 microarchitecture, Mike Clark agreed with the sentiment: “I’m not going to mislead and act like I knew agentic computing was coming, or we knew it, but we did strongly believe that the CPU would always have a place in the software ecosystem of AI… And as agentic [has] blown up, we [are] able to now deploy that for agentic compute workloads.”

As AMD rolls out its next-generation microarchitecture with Venice CPUs, it’s targeting the messaging from Arm and Nvidia, which have posed the most direct threat to AMD’s continued rise in the data center on the back of agentic workloads. And indeed, there are performance vectors where Nvidia’s Vera looks strong for agentic workloads, as you can see in our Nvidia Vera testing. AMD’s argument, however, is that agentic AI calls for a broad range of CPUs just as general-purpose workloads do, pushing back on the single-SKU approach of Nvidia and Arm.

Our testing of two AMD Venice chips supports that argument, as well, with the dense EPYC 9996 with its 256 cores and 512 threads offering peak throughput performance, while the frequency-optimized EPYC 9686F goes toe-to-toe with Vera in the role of an AI host node. AMD is launching two additional ranges next year, with Venice-X targeting HPC with its 3D-stack cache, and Verano going directly after Vera as an AI host node with an LPDDR5X memory system.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Although the workload characteristics of agentic AI are still in the process of being defined, we have a better understanding of how those loops work now than we did at the beginning of the year. And like all complex topics, the answer to what is important in a CPU for agentic AI is the always-disappointing “it depends.”

As we break down in our early testing procedure for agentic AI, the workload characteristics and demand on the CPU/memory are defined by what the agent is doing, and in the data center, what several agents are doing at high concurrency when sandboxed. A coding agent will generally call more tools that are taxing on the CPU than a web browsing agent, for example, while an agent that queries a database may call for strong memory performance.

Of course, there are some vectors of performance that can’t be addressed with different SKUs (such as that strong memory performance). However, the potential diversity of agentic workloads certainly makes an argument for CPU variety. At a most basic level, an agentic loop allows the LLM to understand functions you’ve defined, and then pass arguments to those functions to execute them. We’re talking about a workload as broad as what you can define in a function in languages like Python and Rust.

AMD winds up Zen 6 cores for faster clock speeds

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Chips boast up to 256 cores, clocks up to 5.3 GHz, and IPC gains of 20 to 31 percent, just not all on the same SKU

After teasing its 256-core Epycs for more than a year, AMD is finally ready to talk about architectural changes to the Zen core that not only make it possible, but will eventually power its next generation of Ryzen processors in desktop and mobile PCs.

During a press event in Las Vegas last week, AMD spilled the beans on its latest Zen cores, which promise an average 27 percent uplift in instructions per clock (IPC) across a variety of AI and server workloads, along with boosted clocks ranging from 4.1 to 5.3 GHz. We expect to see even higher frequencies on consumer chips before long, but AMD isn’t quite ready to talk about Ryzen just yet.

CPU performance is a function of power, IPC, and clocks, and with gains across all three, the cores are shaping up to be one of the company’s biggest generational jumps ever.

As in past Epyc launches, Zen 6 isn’t one core. It comes in two flavors: a frequency-optimized variant (Zen 6) and a density-optimized version (Zen 6c).

This split dates back to the launch of AMD’s Bergamo Epycs in 2023, which packed 128 compact Zen 4c cores into just eight chiplets. The chip’s debut drew comparisons to Intel’s still relatively new Efficient (E-cores) for obvious reasons. But under the hood, AMD’s Zen 4c was nothing like what Intel was doing. The core itself was nearly identical to Zen 4; it simply traded clocks and a bit of cache per core for a more densely packed CPU die.

The only difference between running an application on Bergamo or Genoa was how quickly it executed. The same could not be said of Intel’s P and E cores, which used different ISAs.

This remained a defining characteristic and advantage of AMD’s compact core architecture through Zen 5, which allowed it to ship Turin parts with up to 192 cores without users having to worry about whether their app was running on a compact core or not.

With Zen 6c, at least as we understand it, this philosophy is largely unchanged. Both Zen 6 and Zen 6c share the same ISA, with clock being the main differentiator. There are other micro-architectural differences between the two cores designed to balance IPC and clock speeds more effectively.

Looking at the core’s block diagram, this isn’t immediately obvious. Zen 6 and Zen 5 share a very similar front and backend.

We'd be tempted to call the core iterative if it weren't for the 20 to 31 percent IPC uplift for cloud and AI workloads that AMD claims over Zen 5. This suggests that core for core, a 4.1 GHz Zen 6c-based Venice Epyc should deliver performance on par with a Zen 5-based Turin Epyc running at 5 GHz.

That's a pretty sizable jump, but just because AMD is seeing generational performance uplift in enterprise server and cloud workloads doesn't mean we'll see the same gains on the client side. Many will recall the roughly 5 percent performance gains gaming PC enthusiasts reported with the Ryzen 9000-series, leading to the rather unfortunate turn of phrase "Zen 5 percent."

That happened because game engines at the time simply weren't CPU-bottlenecked, and so faster cores could only raise the ceiling so much. Even so, AMD fellow and "Zen daddy" Mike Clark tells El Reg that PC buyers expect more than a 6 percent gain from Zen 6.

We're glossing over a bunch of low-level architectural optimizations here, but according to Clark, one of the biggest drivers of performance was the front end. Zen 5 introduced a dual front end, which for simple workloads worked great, but lost a lot of steam when confronted with more dynamic loads.

"The Zen 6 team did a really good job for those larger footprint workloads that have a bipolar nature," Clark said. "It's not something that comes through. You're not going to see it in a block diagram very well. It's sort of subtle... but it's a big lever."

Another contributor was the move to a distributed scheduler in the out-of-order pipeline. One of the biggest performance drivers in modern CPU cores is that they don't execute instructions in the order they receive them. Instead, they reorder the instructions so as to keep the core's backend execution units fed with a constant stream of instructions.

This is where we see the biggest difference between Zen 6 and Zen 6c. To account for the slower clocks on the latter core, AMD gave the core's scheduler deeper queues, which Clark tells us helps with higher-latency workloads. Meanwhile, from what we understand, Zen 6's shallower queue enables it to clock higher on less power.

Finally, on the backend, AMD made several subtle changes which should have major implications for vector heavy workloads, including AI. One of the more significant was the ability to perform four AVX2 or two AVX-512 loads per cycle.

While this doesn't improve AVX-512 performance much, for applications that haven't been updated to use it, the core is now able to perform twice as many loads, which should improve throughput for legacy applications. This should come in handy, particularly on PCs, where Intel's outright lack of AVX-512 support has made AVX2 the lowest common denominator for a lot of software.

That's not to say AVX-512 didn't get some love this generation. For those running AI workloads on CPUs, Zen 6 now natively supports FP16 datatypes. Previously FP16 was supported, but required padding the data to FP32, which meant the lower precision didn't buy much in terms of performance. That's no longer the case in Zen 6.

And while we're on the topic of AI acceleration, Clark notes that Zen 6 doubled the VNNI-INT8 pipes from two to four, and should effectively double throughput for workloads that support it.

While not strictly part of the core, AMD's Zen 6 processors also address one of the biggest asymmetries associated with its frequency and density optimized cores.

Going back to Bergamo, opting for a density-optimized Epyc didn't just come at the expense of clocks, but cache too. Each Zen 4c or Zen 5c chiplet contained twice as many cores, but the same amount of L3 cache.

16 channels of DDR5, 5 GHz clock speeds, and support for CXL 3.1 memory god boxes? What's not to like?

With Zen 6 and Zen 6c, you may be giving up some clocks, but cache per core is now a constant 4 MB. As we discussed in a Venice deep dive back in September, each of the processor's eight CPU dies contains 12 or 32 cores and 48 and 128 MB respectively.

The benefit here is that users don't have to worry about different ISAs or cache hierarchies when optimizing their software stacks against AMD's Epyc processors. The same cannot be said of Intel's Xeon processors.

As we alluded to earlier, Chipzilla's performance and efficiency cores use different micro-architectures, ISAs, cache hierarchies, clocks, and even cluster their cores differently. This can pose real headaches if you have workloads that take advantage of fat vector registers like AVX-512, or accelerators like AMX, which will work just fine on Intel's P-cores but won't even run on their E-cores.

This, along with support for SMT (Hyperthreading), which Intel has dropped for now, could help the House of Zen vacuum up even more market share from its long-time rival. ®

X Previews Labels That Tell Users Why Its AI System Flagged Their Posts

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

X’s AI-powered content moderation system once mistakenly labeled a post featuring an image of a SpaceX rocket launch as “adult content.” No one, including the author of the post, could see why.

That’s the problem Michael O’Herlihy, a Twitter veteran who recently rejoined X as head of safety, wants to fix.

In the future, X’s content moderation systems may show their decisioning process, he suggested at the Brand & AI Safety Summit in New York on Tuesday (Nov. 10). There, O’Herlihy previewed designs for labels displayed beneath content on X that explain what its AI detected in a post, detail how exactly the post’s reach was limited after being flagged, and allow users to appeal the decision.

Christensen Arms Ridgeline FFT in 300 PRC Rifle, Expert Tested and Reviewed

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

This article was produced in partnership with Christensen Arms

We’ve looked at the Christensen Arms Ridgeline FFT rifle in the past. In fact, it was selected as our editor’s choice during our annual 2025 rifle test. The rifle is offered in a plethora of cartridges, and while cartridge choice is not the most important aspect in a rifle a hunter might plan to use all over the world, it does matter. Several rifle cartridges qualify as world-wide big game cartridges, and while magnum performance is not mandatory, many prefer that extra power for assurance at distance, especially for larger game. With that as a guiding principle I decided to revisit the Christensen Arms Ridgeline FFT chambered in 300 PRC to see how it might fill this anywhere and everything category.

The two most distinguishing characteristics of the Ridgeline FFT are its carbon fiber wrapped barrel and its carbon fiber stock. What many hunters and shooters do not realize is that Christensen Arms pioneered the carbon fiber wrapped barrel. The Ridgeline’s barrel is made of 416 stainless-steel, and it is button-rifled and hand-honed at the factory. Christensen Arms also contours the barrel with a proprietary stepped process that reduces in diameter from the ends to the center, then they wrapped the barrel various layers of carbon fiber. In 300 PRC the barrel is 22 inches long, has a 1 in 8 rifling twist, and is threaded at 5/8×24. A thread protector is provided but the rifle ships with a stainless steel side baffle muzzle brake.

The carbon stock—offered in a variety of cool looking camo patterns—on the Ridgeline FFT is just as proprietary as the barrel and Christensen Arms builds them at their factory in Gunnison, Utah. FFT stands for “Flash Forged Technology,” and this stock is of a monocoque design, which means there is no internal support structure. It’s outfitted with carbon fiber pillars that the action screws pass through, it’s glass bedded in the recoil lug area, and is relieved to provide a free-floating barrel. With the buttpad installed the stock weighs only about 20 ounces, which is light even by modern synthetic stock standards. It also has a hinged aluminum floor plate but is compatible with aftermarket AICS detachable magazine conversion kits.

The Ridgeline FFT utilizes Christensen Arms Model 14 action, which is a variation on the Remington 700 action, and it will accept Remington 700 scope bases and mounts. This is a two-lug action with a 90° throw, but that’s where the similarities end. The bolt release on the Model 14 action is located on the left side of the action, the bolt is spiral fluted, and its outfitted with a much more robust and reliable M16-style extractor. Inside the bolt face there are redundant—two—spring loaded plunger ejectors. The handle on the bolt is oversized and grooved, and the bolt handle is skeletonized for looks and to reduce weight. And finally, out of the box this rifle comes with a very good TriggerTech trigger that’s user adjustable. Every Ridgeline FFT rifle also comes with Christensen Arms sub-MOA accuracy guarantee.

From a muzzle velocity standpoint, the 300 Precision Rifle Cartridge (PRC) falls about in the middle of Magnum 0.30-caliber rifle cartridges. Introduced in 2018 by Hornady, the 300 PRC is not the fastest 0.30 caliber. It has about a 50 to 100 fps advantage over the 300 Winchester Magnum but it’s about 100 to 150 fps behind the 300 Weatherby Magnum and the 300 Remington Ultra Magnum, respectively. However, the 300 PRC performs very well at distance because of its 1 turn in 8.5-inch rifling twist rate which allows it to shoot very aero dynamic high ballistic coefficient bullets that retain velocity better and drift less in the wind.

For example, zeroed at 200 yards Hornady’s 212-grian ELD-X load for the 300 PRC will only drop about 38 inches at 500 yards, where it will still impact at 2200 fps and with 2304 foot pounds of kinetic energy. A 200-grain bullet from the 300 Winchester Magnum cannot match this, and you must step up to the 27% harder recoiling 220-grain bullet in a 300 Remington Ultra Magnum to match this performance. This all makes the 300 PRC a great choice for hunters who want to deliver maximum power at distance. There’s also about 20 factory loads offered for the 300 PRC.

I mounted one of the new Zeiss V3 riflescopes to the supplied 20 MOA rail that comes with the rifle using Leupold Backcountry rings. Even then, the rifle weighed less than seven pounds. Four loads were tested by firing three, five-shot groups from a sandbag rest at 100 yards with each load. In total, 12, five-shot groups were fired and the average for all those groups was 1.23 inches. All this shooting was conducted with the side baffle muzzle brake installed.

Christensen offers a sub-MOA precision guarantee with the Ridgeline FFT rifle, and this translates to an assurance the rifle can put three shots into a group measuring less than roughly an inch at 100 yards. Of course, as with any precision guarantee this assumes a capable shooter is behind the rifle and that quality ammunition is being used. Our standard precision testing always relies on five-shot groups, so no three-shot groups were measured. However, in most every case the first three shots with the 190-grain Outfitter and 210-grain Terminal Ascent loads would most likely have fallen within that sub-MOA measurement.

I’m not a fan of muzzle brakes because to me, they’re obnoxiously loud and disconcerting, and the one provided by Christensen was all of that. But at the expense of excessive muzzle blast, it was very effective at reducing recoil to what I would consider 30-06 Springfield and maybe even 308 Winchester levels. That’s notable considering the 300 PRC generates more than twice the recoil force of both cartridges. I also did some shooting with two different suppressors which also reduced recoil, but neither offered as much relief as the muzzle brake.

The Ridgeline FFT is balanced well for field shooting, either off hand or from shooting sticks. The action was smooth and it fed, fired and ejected every load tried with perfect reliability. I had two complaints with the rifle. The first is that in 300 PRC it has a limited magazine capacity of two, which allows for a fully loaded capacity of three. The second complaint is that the two position safety does not lock the bolt handle down when it’s in the “safe” position.

During a visit to the factory a few years back I got to assist with building a Ridgeline FFT chambered for the 6.5 PRC. That rifle and I have spent a lot of time together on the range and in the field. In fact, I took it to Africa in 2025 and it’s the rifle my wife used. I used another Ridgeline FFT chambered in 7mm Backcountry. So, I’ve got some experience hunting with the platform. Aside from the non-bolt locking safety I think the Ridgeline FFT is one of the best modern lightweight bolt action rifles currently offered. It delivers a great balance of performance and weight, especially at its price point. There’s no question that one in 300 PRC could effectively serve a big game hunter all over the world, and if the 300 PRC is not your cup of tea there are near two dozen other chamberings to choose from.

Forget Mayo: Spread This Rich Ingredient On Bread For A More Flavorful Grilled Cheese

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Butter gives your grilled cheese great richness, and using mayo makes it far crispier, but neither adds a ton of strong flavors that can shine through high-quality cheese. That's why you need toum, a Lebanese garlic spread that delivers intense taste with very little effort.

Toum is as rich as mayonnaise and as light and fluffy as whipped cream, created by smashing garlic into a paste and then gradually whipping in oil until it has an airy but glossy consistency. For texture, its incredible fluffiness makes it easy to slather on, covering every last inch in a swiftly melting browning agent that makes the bread crispy. But for flavoring, it makes the end product taste like garlic bread, full of rich fats and pungent aromatics that only requires a swift spread with a knife. It may seem like you're using a lot and will over-season your sandwich, but since so much of it is air, you're actually only using a moderate amount that's quite easy to thoroughly apply.

The combination of melty gooeyness inside and crisp, garlicky exteriors is a second-to-none combination that pairs well with a wide variety of cheeses. Add in the sharpness of the lemon juice, and you get something that can brighten up even the heaviest, fat-laden cheeses quite well. The only caveat is that all that garlic gives it a fairly cool smoke point, so you'll need to use low to medium heat to avoid making it grow bitter.

Since its main flavors are garlic and lemon, toum isn't quite as universal a pairing as mayonnaise or butter. It goes great with anything semi-hard and creamy, but its intensity may clash with anything stronger. You'll also want to know the type of oil used in your toum. Something made with grapeseed, canola, or other neutral varieties is far more compatible than a spread made with a flavorful extra virgin olive oil.

Cheeses with a nuttier profile, like Jarlsberg, Gruyère, and Manchego, are all solid pairings. They tend to be a bit earthier, grounding the brightness of the toum, so it isn't too intense. Since it has no eggs, you can even combine it with melted vegan cheese since nutritional yeast is typically quite aromatic and nutty. Richer, well-rounded varieties can also be quite nice, so some shreds of gouda, raclette, and any triple-cream variety are always welcome additions.

Flavored cheeses are one of the easiest ways to upgrade any melty sandwich, but not all are compatible with toum. While something rich in herbs or wine can complement the garlic quite well, a variety with a lot of sweetened fruit or more lemon can be a bit much. Since toum is already quite tangy, you may also want to steer clear of acidic cheeses like chèvre, feta, or cream cheese. While some may like the brightness, it can quickly become overly intense, and you may find yourself making a new sandwich.

Man United Confidential: Jim Ratcliffe makes new 'brutal' cutbacks to ex-players' charity, Nathan Collins and Calvin Bassey among January targets, Luke Shaw eyes Anfield return and why club confiscated two anti-owner banners

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

By CHRIS WHEELER, NORTHERN SPORTS WRITER and NATHAN SALT - FOOTBALL REPORTER

Published: 12:15 EST, 10 November 2026 | Updated: 16:44 EST, 10 November 2026

Sir Jim Ratcliffe is known for being ruthless – as Michael Carrick may be about to find out – and it appears as though the Association of Former Manchester United Players has once again paid the price.

Two years ago, Ratcliffe made front-page news at Christmas after it emerged he was cutting a yearly £40,000 donation to the AFMUP charity, which has been helping ex-United players since 1985.

Last week organisers were informed at the last minute that the second of its two annual dinners couldn’t be staged as usual in the Manchester Suite at Old Trafford and would have to move elsewhere.

So while plans were being made for Sir Alex Ferguson’s glittering 40th anniversary bash in the Heritage Suite on Friday night, the AFMUP had to rely on Hotel Football – part-owned by Gary Neville and the Class of ’92 – coming to the rescue to host their event over the road 24 hours earlier. Ferguson, it has to be said, has been a big supporter of the charity over the years.

There are now concerns that AFMUP will never be allowed back to Old Trafford with United understood to be insisting on a total of 400 guests, which is double what the charity can provide.

Previously, the AFMUP gave half of the £40,000 donation back to the club to hire the suite in April and September, raising additional funds through ticket sales and auctions.

Sir Jim Ratcliffe (left) is known for being ruthless and it appears as though the Association of Former Manchester United Players has once again paid the price

When Neville challenged Ratcliffe over the ‘cruel decision’ to cut the donation in a one-on-one interview in March last year and said the club should consider organising the dinner itself and asking current players to attend, the minority owner replied: ‘Well nobody suggested that, I would have supported that.’

As a result, United chief executive Omar Berrada joined Ferguson and former CEO David Gill at the dinner the following month – when Neville, Andy Cole and Jonny Evans were also present – and donated a VIP package for the auction worth £20,000.

Director of football Jason Wilcox then appeared at the next event in September and gave a rare interview on stage. In April of this year, Bryan Robson and Steve Bruce formed a popular double act as the main attraction.

Thursday’s dinner, which had already been moved from September 24 due to a double booking, was a celebration of the life of Duncan Edwards, who would have been 90 on October 1. Former chairman Martin Edwards and Gill were both present along with former goalkeeper Alex Stepney.

The AFMUP’s official filmmaker and publicist John Gubba highlighted the latest setback on his Man Utd The Religion YouTube channel, saying: ‘While the red carpet is being rolled out for Fergie, the club have been brutally abandoning the former players’ association.

‘The latest cutbacks by Sir Jim Ratcliffe have resulted in another kick in the teeth for the AFMUP. Staging their big event at the Theatre of Dreams was always a status that made the former players and their guests feel part of the football club. Being turfed out at short notice leaves a bitter taste.’ You can watch the full video here.

United sources say the club helped to facilitate an alternative venue at Hotel Football due to unavailability in the stadium, and insist that the charity will be welcome back to Old Trafford in future.

Berrada was present at Ferguson’s celebration dinner on Friday in which the United chief executive spoke about the importance of building a culture at Old Trafford.

He was one of 100 invited guests for the event to mark 40 years since Ferguson was appointed as United manager, moving from Aberdeen to replace Ron Atkinson in November 1986.

A number of Ferguson’s old players were present and posed for pictures with him including Michael Carrick, Brian McClair, Edwin van der Sar, Robin van Persie, David May, Wes Brown, Paul Parker, Dion Dublin, Denis Irwin, Jesper Blomqvist, Ryan Giggs, Gary Pallister, Javier Hernandez, Clayton Blackmore, Jonny Evans, John O’Shea, Darren Fletcher, Ronny Johnsen, Lee Martin, Nemanja Vidic and Quinton Fortune.

Ferguson’s former No2 Mike Phelan was also there along with Gill and kit man Albert Morgan.

There were 100 invited guests for the event to mark 40 years since Sir Alex Ferguson (third right) was appointed as United manager in November 1986

Luke Shaw is targeting a return to action away at Liverpool a week on Sunday, Confidential understands.

The 31-year-old has not featured since October 10 when he played 90 minutes against Tottenham, missing seven games through to the current international break.

That absence badly exposed United's lack of depth at left back, an area the hierarchy failed to address in the transfer window despite a clear need. Diogo Dalot, who was targeted by fans when he came off against Aston Villa at Old Trafford on Sunday, has played poorly while filling in on his unfavoured side.

With Patrick Dorgu now back in the mix, too, Shaw is hoping to be in contention for the trip to Anfield in what would be a timely boost for a defence that has conceded the joint-most goals in all competitions of any team in Europe’s top five leagues this season.

Nothing has undermined Carrick's time as United head coach more than the defensive problems that have seen his team concede 15 goals in their last five games.

United chose to delay the overhaul of their defence until next year when they focused on rebuilding the midfield last summer, but it has proved to be a costly misjudgment with Matthijs de Ligt only just returning to action after a year out and a spate of injuries to Shaw, Lisandro Martinez, Dorgu and Noussair Mazraoui.

It may force United into the transfer market in January and Confidential understands Brentford centre back Nathan Collins and Fulham’s Calvin Bassey have joined Jarrad Branthwaite of Everton on the list of potential targets.

United also want a striker after failing to offload Joshua Zirkzee and sign cover for Benjamin Sesko in the summer, but a loan deal is more likely.

Brentford's Nathan Collins and Fulham's Nigeria star Calvin Bassey have both joined United's list of centre-back targets

As if the prospect of relegation isn’t nightmarish enough for United after they dropped to 18th place in the table, the cost of going down would be astronomical.

Although parachute payments from the Premier League would cushion the fall in the unlikely event that United are relegated, kit sponsors adidas have the option to scrap their record £900million, 10-year deal which was announced in 2023 but only came into effect last year.

Under the terms of the contract, the German sportswear giant can cancel it if they give the club one year’s notice. Adidas can also halve their annual payment to £45m if United aren’t playing in the Premier League, and hit the club with a £10m annual penalty if they are not in the Champions League.

Two banners protesting against United’s owners were confiscated by stewards during Sunday’s 4-4 draw with Aston Villa at Old Trafford.

One that read ‘Jim Can’t Fix This’ was seized because its connection to Jimmy Savile was deemed to be inappropriate. Another declaring ‘We Don’t Want A New Stadium, We Want New Owners’ was taken down because it was blocking the view of some fans.

Club insiders say protests will always be allowed as long as they remain within the regulations. United kept a close eye on Sunday’s demonstration with a more visible police presence in the Stretford End and stewards asking fans as they entered that section of the stadium which group they were with.

The protest, which once again involved green and gold balloons being released before kick-off, was jointly organised by The Red Army, The 1958, Manchester United Youth Supporters Club, South Stand United and Retro Reds.

However, it seemed like a strange decision to confiscate the banners considering that United were in possession of them for more than 30 hours before kick-off since 10am on Saturday morning.

They are stored in the same place in the Stretford End before every game, so it was odd for United to demand that they be taken down when they could have refused for them to be handed out in the first place.

A banner protesting against United's owners was confiscated during the 4-4 draw with Aston Villa

While goalkeeper Senne Lammens was giving his worst performance in a United shirt against Villa on Sunday, the man he replaced at Old Trafford was faring a little better in Turkey.

Andre Onana kept a clean sheet in Trabzonspor’s win over Alanyaspor as they moved into second place in the Super Lig table behind Galatasaray.

It came three days after a Europa League defeat by Freiburg when Onana couldn’t resist a little light ribbing of English reporters at the game.

Asked by journalists from Daily Mail Sport and the BBC for a quick word, the Cameroon international didn’t disappoint. ‘I don’t speak English, bro,’ Onana said, in perfect English. Classic.

Nebul Selects NetApp Novus to Scale Its Sovereign AI Cloud

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

NetApp announced on November 10, 2026 that Nebul, a European AI-native cloud provider, is the first neocloud to adopt its Novus storage architecture for sovereign AI infrastructure. Nebul will use Novus in its sovereign Intelligent Cloud. The architecture is generally available and designed for up to 100 TB per second of aggregate throughput.

Nebul describes its Intelligent Cloud as a sovereign platform combining accelerated infrastructure, enterprise data services and production AI capabilities while preserving customer ownership and control. Beyond the adoption, the companies are exploring opportunities to accelerate sovereign-AI uptake by bringing together Nebul’s services and the NetApp Platform.

For Nebul’s multi-tenant environment, NetApp says Novus can scale performance, capacity and concurrency independently beneath one namespace as demand grows. Nebul founder and CEO Arnold Juffer said: “With this architecture, we can scale our AI Factory to serve growing customer demand without sacrificing the performance, security or compliance they depend on.”

Novus separates metadata, the information that maps file locations, from movement of the data itself. NetApp’s technical explanation describes a client requesting a layout from Novus Data Director and then communicating directly with ONTAP-based storage. Data Director is an independently scalable, software-defined control layer running on standard x86 compute, allowing the metadata and data planes to scale separately. This addresses the conflict that arises when small-file metadata operations and large checkpoint writes share controllers.

The Novus product documentation says the system works with standard in-kernel NFSv4.2 and pNFS Flex Files clients using nconnect, plus NVIDIA GPUDirect Storage. Operators therefore do not need custom client software across thousands of GPU servers. Additional ONTAP clusters add throughput without remounts or fragmentation, while every GPU continues to see the same namespace.

The ONTAP foundation also carries the data-governance side of the announcement. NetApp says the design is intended to let organizations using its platform extend existing data estates into sovereign cloud environments while preserving consistent security, protection and governance, including current jurisdiction and access policies. Willem Hendrickx, NetApp’s senior vice president and general manager for EMEA and LATAM, identified government, healthcare and biotech as regulated industries where providers must address compliance, security and multi-tenancy.

NetApp introduced Novus on September 29, 2026, making the architecture orderable that day. The initial release combines Data Director software on qualified Supermicro infrastructure with ONTAP data services on AFF A90 systems. NetApp described a path toward software-defined deployments over time while maintaining the same namespace, operating model and data foundation.

NetApp’s economic argument is that GPUs waiting on storage still depreciate without generating a return. At the September introduction, the company said utilization can fall below 30% under traditional architectures when storage cannot supply data quickly enough. The launch therefore framed storage throughput as a direct constraint on GPU-cluster economics.

NetApp technical fellow Arindam Banerjee’s explanation illustrates the bandwidth requirement with one GPU demanding as much as 2 GB per second and 50,000 GPUs requiring that rate simultaneously. It puts conventional storage arrays at 40–80 GB per second; reaching Novus’s aggregate target with such arrays would require more than 100 systems, each adding a namespace, failure domain and management overhead. The comparison connects the architecture’s single namespace to the operational overhead of scaling storage.

For Nebul, the adoption will test whether independently scaling metadata and data can support more tenants and GPUs without fragmenting data across storage systems, while jurisdiction, access and governance controls remain consistent. The operational challenge is to expand shared AI infrastructure without weakening the isolation and control that regulated customers require.

Charta Health Raises $55M Series B to Expand AI Chart Review

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Charta Health announced on November 10, 2026, that it had raised a $55 million Series B to expand its AI chart-review technology. Insight Partners led the financing, which also drew QuantumLight and returning investors Madrona, Bain Capital Ventures and South Park Commons.

Charta will put the capital toward further AI development for provider customers, entry into additional specialties and its goal of establishing one trusted interpretation of a chart before billing. Alongside the financing, it broadened Charta for Payers with a suite initially aimed at Medicare Advantage and Affordable Care Act Marketplace plans.

Charta says its provider software reviews every chart before billing against coding guidelines, payer rules and each customer’s clinical protocols. Findings appear for human approval inside the electronic health record, leaving staff to validate the issues flagged by the system.

The software applies large language models to unstructured material such as free-text notes, scanned orders, laboratory results and images. Its task is to establish what care was delivered and whether the documentation supports the billing, moving accuracy checks ahead of the denials and appeals that can follow a claim.

Compliance and medical leaders can also examine documentation deficiencies, error patterns and provider performance across patient encounters. American Family Care, Grow Therapy and Pediatric Associates Family of Companies are among the healthcare enterprises using the technology. Charta reports consistent double-digit reductions in denial rates for fee-for-service billing.

The payer products look for documented conditions that were not coded and for coded diagnoses that lack support in the chart. Their six product areas span retrospective diagnosis coding, automated second-pass coding, a smart coding workspace, prospective risk-adjustment intelligence, diagnosis validation and audit workflow, and quality care-gap detection. Charta says it designed the suite with one of the nation’s largest health insurers, without naming the partner.

The payer platform extracts structured clinical and encounter intelligence from records that plans already retrieve, allowing the same evidence to support retrospective coding, second-pass quality assurance, audit work and prospective workflows. Charta says its automated second-pass product applies AI to 100% of coded charts, while the smart coding workspace presents supporting evidence, citations, and additions and deletions for validation in one review.

For retrospective risk adjustment, AI presents clinical evidence to human coders, who use customer-specific guidelines to validate additional diagnoses and flag unsupported codes for deletion. Smart Scan pre-screens the chart population to identify records with enough potential value to warrant full review. Charta advertises 98% accuracy for diagnosis coding in this workflow, based on the combination of AI evidence recall and human validation.

Insight Partners co-founder and managing director Jeff Horing pointed to Charta’s pairing of AI and customer-specific coding expertise across providers and payers. “As payers and providers both adopt AI, we believe the opportunity lies in getting the underlying record, or the ‘ground truth,’ right before a claim is submitted,” he said.

The new round follows Charta’s $22 million Series A, announced on July 22, 2025 and led by Bain Capital Ventures, with Madrona, SV Angel, Refract Ventures and South Park Commons participating. Charta said its total funding exceeded $30 million at that time. Where the earlier announcement focused on automating provider chart review, this one pairs continued provider development with a broader payer product rollout.

The expansion makes a shared chart record the central test of Charta’s model. The same evidence can help a provider submit a better-supported claim and help a payer identify an unsupported diagnosis; the commercial challenge is making that review dependable enough for both sides while humans remain responsible for the coding decisions.

Travel interest is slipping, but revenue is holding because prices are doing the work. That cushion only lasts as long as travelers keep paying more, which makes falling search activity worth watching closely heading into 2027.

Travel demand signals softened in September 2026 while pricing kept revenue intact. The Skift Travel Health Index stood at 99, down 1% year-on-year, and the destinations index, which tracks travel interest through online search activity, fell 12% to 88 as inbound flight and hotel searches weakened across most tracked markets. Inbound flight search growth was negative in the majority of the 21 destinations tracked, with the United Arab Emirates down 37%, Hong Kong down 18%, Australia down 16%, and Mexico down 15%, while China (+11%), India (+7%), and Italy (+5%) gained. Intent data points the same way: the European Travel Commission found the share of global travelers planning a long-haul trip fell from 55% in autumn 2025 to 50% in autumn 2026, and interest in visiting Europe slipped from 38% to 34%. The share of travelers who had already booked flights stayed broadly unchanged, so the cooling is concentrated at the top of the funnel. Higher airfares and accommodation rates are holding up revenue as traveler interest cools.

Airline Interline, Codeshare, Alliance, And Joint Venture Agreements Explained

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Airline partnerships are more complex and advanced than ever before, both for better and worse. About 25 years ago the Star Alliance was formed, which was revolutionary at the time. SkyTeam and oneworld quickly joined the scene.

While they all still exist, the global alliances have definitely been deemphasized a bit in recent years. After all, with these alliances having so many members, how much can loosely affiliated airlines really have in common?

Airlines want to work with other airlines that complement them best, and this can come in many different forms. Just to give a few examples of partnerships outside of the traditional alliances:

Airlines have several different levels of cooperation, ranging from interline agreements, to codeshare agreements, to alliance membership, to joint venture agreements. In this post, I’d like to take a closer look at the differences between these agreements. Before I do so, let me add two disclaimers:

With that out of the way, let’s take a look at these four kinds of agreements.

Interline agreements are the most basic kinds of agreements you can have between airlines. An interline agreement is simply a commercial agreement between airlines to handle passengers when they’re traveling on multiple airlines on the same itinerary, and it allows an airline to issue a ticket on another airline (though not necessarily on any sort of favorable commercial terms).

This allows passengers to check their bags through to their final destination, get boarding passes all way to their destination, potentially be rebooked on another airline in the event of irregular operations, etc.

This is a basic level of cooperation, so there are airlines that have interline agreements but don’t otherwise have robust partnerships. For example, Etihad doesn’t belong to a major global alliance, but has an interline agreement with dozens of airlines, ranging from American, to Emirates, to JetBlue, to United, to Qatar Airways.

As you can see, this is a level of cooperation we even see between airlines that don’t partner with one another, and that may not even be fond of one another.

It is worth noting that in some cases airlines specifically pull out of interline agreements. For example, many years ago Delta cut its interline agreement with American. Why? At least in part it’s because Delta found that American was rebooking more passengers on Delta during irregular operations than the other way around, so the carrier no longer thought the arrangement was in its best interest.

A codeshare agreement is the next level of cooperation between airlines. This is when two airlines realize there’s value in working together, and they decide they want to place their “codes” on one another’s flights. Typically the main benefit of this is that it drums up business for the airlines that are in a codeshare agreement with one another.

Just to give an example, Alaska Airlines and Icelandair have a codeshare agreement. Icelandair flies to certain gateways in the United States, and then Alaska Airlines provides some of the “feed” for those flights in the form of codeshares.

For example, if you want to fly from Reykjavik to Los Angeles, Icelandair doesn’t operate that route. However, Icelandair flies to Portland, and then you can book a Portland to Los Angeles flight on Alaska Airlines as an Icelandair codeshare.

When you book a codeshare flight, you’ll notice that the flight number is different. So if you booked through Icelandair, your Portland to Los Angeles flight would also have an Icelandair flight number (in this case FI7696). To be clear:

With these codeshare agreements, generally neither airline is getting a huge cut for booking someone on the other airline. Most of the ticket revenue goes to the operating carrier, and often a small amount goes to the airline that sold the flight. However, the biggest advantage of this arrangement is to boost business for both airlines by increasing the number of flights that passengers have access to.

The “big three” alliances — oneworld, SkyTeam, and Star Alliance — have only developed in the past 25 years, though in the meantime many major global carriers belong to one of them. The basic way that alliances work is as follows:

I’d note that different airlines place different levels of emphasis on these alliances, even if they choose to belong to them. For example, Delta is a founding member of SkyTeam, but the airline is heavily focused on joint ventures and airlines it owns a stake in, and cares very little about the airlines it simply shares an alliance with.

If a codeshare agreement is like dating, then a joint venture is like getting married. A joint venture agreement is a massive business decision that typically requires extensive government approval. When airlines form a joint venture they coordinate pricing and schedules, and have a revenue sharing agreement.

Exactly how the revenue sharing works depends on the specific arrangement, but the idea is that two airlines are essentially acting as one under a joint venture.

Note that typically airlines form joint ventures between specific regions, which is why this is different than an outright merger. For example, American has a transatlantic joint venture with British Airways, Finnair, Iberia, and Aer Lingus, while the airline has two transpacific joint ventures, with both Japan Airlines and Qantas (the two are separate arrangements).

From the perspective of a consumer, a joint venture is both good and bad. The good news is that typically it gives you the most flight options in terms of schedules, since the airlines are operating as one. Airlines also often try to make the experience as consistent across brands as possible. The downside is that it’s like a competitor being eliminated in the market, so it could lead to higher fares as it reduces competition.

There are so many different types of agreements in the airline industry nowadays. While the exact terms vary with every partnership, I think the easiest way to sum it up is that an interline agreement is like a friendship, a codeshare agreement is like an engagement, a joint venture is like a marriage, and an alliance is like having a big family, with everyone sort of doing their own thing.

There’s a lot more to all of this, but hopefully the above provides a pretty fair and basic summary. People often misuse these terms, so I figure it’s worth recapping every so often.

Australia's Cheapest New Car Is This $13,000 MG Hatchback, And It's Far From A Stripper

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Chinese cars have been beating down on long established performance and luxury car brands for a while now, all while costing a fraction of the price. From jumping over potholes to setting top speed records to beating Land Cruisers in off-road tests and even having emergency water travel capabilities, there are some mighty impressive pieces of kit across the Chinese car industry. What I'm most interested in, though, are the ultra-cheap economy cars that sell for the price of a used shitbox, and that's exactly what the MG3 offers to Australian car buyers. It's a compact hatchback with the face of a plecostomus, which isn't especially exciting, but its $13,020 USD price sure is.

It's no stripper, either. That bargain price includes features like Apple CarPlay and Android Auto, steering wheel controls, air conditioning, a digital driver's display, an auto-hold function, automatic power window switches, and adaptive cruise control with lane centering. It even has a standard feature that the new Toyota RAV4 doesn't even offer on its most expensive trim level that happens to be four times the price of the MG3: padded front and rear armrests. OK, so the MG3's actual MSRP in Australia is $13,890.85 USD at current exchange rates, but the fact that a dealer was even willing to discount it further is remarkable, too.

As you can see from CarSauce's review, the MG3 is woefully slow. Its 1.5-liter four-cylinder engine sends just 109 horsepower and 105 pound-feet of torque to the front wheels via a continuously variable transmission, which results in a leisurely 0-to-62 mph time of 12.57 seconds. It ain't quick, but it gets the job done, and again, it's a $13,000 car. The last car sold in the United States that was near the $13,000 mark was the diminutive Chevrolet Spark, which ran the 0-to-60 sprint in 10.8 seconds in Car and Driver's testing, so the MG3 isn't too far off that pace. Plus, looking beyond straight-line speed (or lack thereof), the reviewer even says it rides great and handles respectably, which are not always the case with bargain-basement cars.

Sadly the MG3's weak engine doesn't equate to exceptional fuel efficiency, with the presenter's real-world economy equating to about 36 mpg on the highway and more like 26 mpg around town. Those aren't abysmal numbers, just perhaps not as good as one might hope from such a weak engine in a tiny car.

Beyond the lower-than-low base price, the MG3's value proposition really comes into focus when you take into account its 10-year manufacturer warranty. For those who say, "why spend $13,000 on the cheapest new car available when you can buy a bigger, better used car for the same price?" Consider the idea of spending $13,000 on a car with a 10-year-long warranty versus spending that same amount on a used car with no warranty. The new car comes with an entire decade of mechanical coverage so the buyer doesn't have to worry about unexpected repair costs for those 10 years, whereas the used car could end up requiring costly repairs within the first year of ownership.

There are some quality concerns noted in the video, including infotainment software glitches and some apparent inconsistencies in the operation of those fancy automatic windows, but considering the price and that stellar warranty, I'd say the pros outweigh the cons in this case. For those who view their cars as another appliance, or those who simply want the financial security of not worrying about repair costs for a whole decade, the MG3 must be a pretty tempting option. Provided you live in Australia, of course — or Mexico, Europe, the Middle East, or other parts of the world where it's pretty damn cheap, too.

Democratic Sweep Puts Crypto Bill On a Cleaner Track

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Home » Regulations » Democratic Sweep Puts Crypto Bill On a Cleaner Track

Regaining the majority at the Senate has shuffled the cards for crypto legislation: will Democrats handle it better?

Democrats’ control of both chambers of Congress has raised fresh questions about how soon Washington can deliver a lasting framework for the digital asset industry.

The stalled CLARITY Act and related market-structure legislation now sit before a Congress that is expected to reopen the terms, priorities, and timing of any overhaul.

The CLARITY Act was meant to settle a core industry question: which federal regulator oversees different parts of the crypto market. Its path was already uncertain.

The new congressional balance adds further negotiation over consumer protections, disclosure rules, clearer exchange oversight, and the split of authority between agencies.

Democrats Want to Rewrite the CLARITY Act and Crypto Could Pay the PriceDemocrats are reportedly preparing a major rewrite of the CLARITY Act after their midterm victory.The proposed changes could give the SEC greater oversight of crypto markets. The language, highlighted by… pic.twitter.com/DepyDC7V2J

Democratic majorities are likely to place a crystal-clear emphasis on investor safeguards and enforcement tools before endorsing a broad framework favored by many digital-asset firms.

Quick legislation is not ruled out, but any significant bill that advances could look different and move more slowly than earlier expectations.

Recently, Federal agencies have continued examining registration pathways and rules for crypto trading platforms even as Congress deliberates.

Crypto firms therefore face an evolving patchwork of agency actions while lawmakers work toward a more permanent statutory settlement.

Industry legal observers now see comprehensive market-structure rules crucial for the CLARITY Act potentially slipping into 2027.

Bitcoin’s recent rebound and renewed inflows into spot Bitcoin ETFs show that institutional interest has held up despite the political uncertainty.

Those ETF flows have offered the market some support, yet they do not resolve the regulatory questions still facing exchanges, token issuers, and decentralized-finance projects.

Market participants can already gain exposure through regulated products while much of the underlying industry waits for clearer operating rules.

A longer legislative process would likely preserve that divide, favoring firms able to absorb compliance costs and adapt to agency guidance.

Natalee Holloway's Mom Recalls 1st Meeting With Daughter's Confessed Killer

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Natalee Holloway’s mom, Beth Holloway, recalled confronting her daughter’s confessed killer, Joran van der Sloot, for the first time after Natalee disappeared.

“My initial interaction with Joran was another moment of, ‘He knows. He knows what happened to her,’” Beth, 65, said during a Monday, November 9, Today show appearance about coming face-to-face with van der Sloot, now 39, upon her arrival in Aruba in 2005.

Van der Sloot has never been charged in connection to Natalee’s murder because Aruba enforces a 12-year statute of limitations for homicides, meaning the window to convict him expired by the time he confessed.

While Beth felt strongly that van der Sloot was responsible for her daughter’s death, he did not admit to killing 18-year-old Natalee for nearly two decades.

Natalee went missing on May 30, 2005, while on a high school graduation trip in Oranjestad, Aruba. She was last seen leaving a nightclub and getting into a car with van der Sloot and brothers Deepak Kalpoe and Satish Kalpoe.

Van der Sloot and the Kalpoe brothers quickly became suspects in the case and were arrested two times amid the investigation, though they were released both times after authorities failed to find enough evidence to charge them.

Five years after Natalee disappeared, van der Sloot was arrested for killing 21-year-old college student Stephany Flores Ramírez. Additionally, he was indicted in the United States on federal wire fraud and extortion charges tied to a March 2010 meeting he had with Beth in which he demanded that she pay him money in exchange for information about her daughter’s disappearance.

Van der Sloot pleaded guilty to the federal extortion charges in 2023 and he was sentenced to serve 20 years in prison, which are running concurrently with his 28-year sentence for Ramírez’s murder. As part of his plea deal, van der Sloot admitted to killing Natalee and agreed to share information about her death.

Beth also reflected on finally learning what happened to her daughter and having the case documented in Netflix’s November docuseries Taken: Natalee Holloway.

“I finally have a conclusion,” she said on the Today Show. “There’s a finale. It took me 18 years to get the answers, but justice takes a lot of work, and I felt like now’s the time to tell, for the first time, a comprehensive and conclusive story of what happened to Natalee and her journey of 20 years.”

Regarding van der Sloot’s confession, Beth said that the “day was a long time coming.”

“The torturous part is the not knowing, and as shocking and as torturous as it is to hear this, it is finally a relief to know that’s what I had been searching for for 18 years,” she said. “I had to know, no matter how brutal it was. I needed to know what happened.”
